Asymptotic safety in the Litim-Sannino model at four loops
A.V. Bednyakov, A.I. Mukhaeva

Abstract
We consider a four-dimensional S ​ U ​ ( N c ) SU(N_{c}) gauge theory coupled to N f N_{f} species of color fermions and N f 2 N_{f}^{2} colorless scalars. The quantum field theory possesses a weakly interacting ultraviolet fixed point that we determine from beta functions computed up to four-loop order in the gauge coupling, and up to three-loop order in the Yukawa and quartic scalar couplings. The fixed point has one relevant direction giving rise to asymptotic safety. We compute fixed point values of dimensionless couplings together with the corresponding scaling exponents up to the first three nontrivial orders in Veneziano parameter ϵ \epsilon , both for infinite and finite number of colors N c N_{c} . We also consider anomalous dimensions for fields, scalar mass squared, and a class of dimension-three operators. Contrary to previous studies, we take into account possible mixing of the latter and compute eigenvalues of the corresponding matrix. Further, we investigate the size of the conformal window in the Veneziano limit and its dependence on N c N_{c} .



I
Introduction

The study of asymptotic behavior of the dimensionless couplings in quantum field theory (QFT) provides important information both for the Standard Model (SM) and Beyond the Standard Model (BSM) scenarios. One of these behaviors is known as asymptotic freedom
Gross and Wilczek 1973
;
Politzer 1973
, which is a defining feature of quantum chromodynamics. This behavior entails a decrease in the value of a coupling with the energy scale. Thus, in the deep ultraviolet (UV), this coupling tends to approach the Gaussian noninteractive fixed point (FP).

Asymptotic safety (AS) is an extension of the concept of asymptotic freedom, as outlined in the work of S. Weinberg
Weinberg 1980
. In AS, the coupling in the deep UV also reaches a fixed point, but unlike in asymptotic freedom, the fixed point value is not zero, which means that the theory remains interactive. Such theories are referred to as asymptotically safe.

The concept of asymptotic safety was initially introduced by S. Weinberg in the late 1970s as a means of achieving nonperturbative renormalizability for the four-dimensional theory of gravity
Weinberg 1980
. However, in recent years, AS has been widely applied in the context of gauge theories to address issues with
U(1)
gauge couplings (Landau pole) by stabilizing them at an interactive fixed point at a certain scale.
Indications of AS has been found in simple
Pica and Sannino 2011
;
Litim and Sannino 2014
, semisimple
Bond and Litim 2018
, and supersymmetric gauge theories coupled to matter
Bajc and Sannino 2016
;
Bond and Litim 2017
;
Bajc et al. 2023
.
From the phenomenological point of view, properties of such UV interactive fixed points for matter fields can be transmitted down to the low-energy regime and lead us to some phenomenological predictions, see, e.g., recent reviews
Eichhorn and Schiffer 2022
;
Bednyakov and Mukhaeva 2023
.

An example of a UV-complete particle theory with a weakly interacting fixed point is a model of
N_{f}
fermions coupled to
SU(N_{c})
gauge fields and elementary scalars through gauge and Yukawa interactions
Antipin et al. 2013a
;
Litim and Sannino 2014
. In the large-
N
Veneziano limit, the fixed point can be systematically studied in perturbation theory using a small control parameter
\epsilon
, allowing for the extraction of specific details of theory. Previous studies
Litim and Sannino 2014
;
Bond et al. 2018
;
Bond et al. 2021
;
Litim et al. 2016
have identified critical couplings and universal exponents up to second order in
\epsilon
, including finite
N
corrections.
Phenomenological applications of the model and its extensions can be found in Refs.
Litim et al. 2016
;
Sannino and Shoemaker 2015
;
Nielsen et al. 2015
;
Rischke and Sannino 2015
;
Codello et al. 2016
;
Bond et al. 2017
;
Dondi et al. 2018
;
Kowalska et al. 2017
;
Abel and Sannino 2017
;
Christiansen et al. 2017
;
Sannino and Skrinjar 2019
;
Barducci et al. 2018
;
Hiller et al. 2020a
;
Hiller et al. 2019
;
Hiller et al. 2020b
;
Bißmann et al. 2021
;
Bause et al. 2022
;
Hiller et al. 2022a
;
Hiller et al. 2022b
. It is also worth mentioning that the model was the first nonsupersymmetric theory investigated in the large global charge limit
Orlando et al. 2019
;
Antipin et al. 2022
. The holographic description of the model was considered in Ref.
Rey and Sannino 2022
.

In this paper, we confirm the study
Litim et al. 2023
of the UV critical theory and provide the fixed point couplings and conformal data up to third order in
\epsilon
(both for infinite and finite-
N_{c}
scenarios) by considering four-loop gauge, three-loop Yukawa, and quartic
\beta
functions. We also determine three-loop anomalous dimensions for dimension-three operators and discuss peculiarities in their computations as compared to Ref.
Litim et al. 2023
.

The paper is organized as follows. Section
II
provides the main information about the considered model and operators. In Sec.
III
we give the details of computation of the renormalization-group (RG) functions. In Sec.
IV
we demonstrate our results for fixed points, anomalous dimensions and scaling exponents for finite
N_{c}
. The bounds on the conformal window are given in Sec.
V
. We conclude in Sec.
VI
. The full expressions for beta functions and various anomalous dimensions can be found in Appendixes
A.1
,
A.2
, and
A.3
. App.
B
contains additional information related to finite-
N_{c}
results.

II
Model description

Field

SU(N_{c})

U_{L}(N_{f})

U_{R}(N_{f})

\psi_{L}

N_{c}

N_{f}

1

\psi_{R}

N_{c}

1

N_{f}

H

1

N_{f}

\bar{N_{f}}

Table 1:
Representations of matter fields under gauge
SU(N_{c})
and flavor

U_{L}(N_{f})
and
U_{R}(N_{f})
groups.

We consider four-dimensional theory with
SU(N_{c})
gauge fields coupled to
N_{f}
massless Dirac fermions and a scalar singlet field
H
.
The last is uncharged under the gauge group and carries two flavor indices, such that it can be written as a
N_{f}\times N_{f}
complex matrix. The corresponding Lagrangian is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}F^{a\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}^{a}+\mathcal{L}_{gf}+\mathcal{L}_{gh}{}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\Tr(\bar{\psi}i\hat{D}\psi)+\Tr(\partial^{\mu}H^{\dagger}\partial_{\mu}H)-y\Tr[\bar{\psi}(H\mathcal{P}_{R}+\ H^{\dagger}\mathcal{P}_{L})\psi]{}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-m^{2}\Tr(H^{\dagger}H)-u\Tr((H^{\dagger}H)^{2})-v(\Tr(H^{\dagger}H))^{2},

(1)

where
F^{a}_{\mu\nu}
is the field strength of the gauge bosons
G^{a}_{\mu}
with
a=1,\ldots,N_{c}^{2}-1
. The trace in Eq. (
1
) runs over both color and flavor indices and
\psi=\psi_{L}+\psi_{R}
are fermions with
\mathcal{P}_{L/R}=\frac{1}{2}(1\pm\gamma_{5})
.
In what follows we use a linear
R_{\xi}
-gauge with
\mathcal{L}_{gf}=-\frac{1}{2\xi}(\partial_{\mu}G^{a}_{\mu})^{2}
together with the corresponding ghost Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}_{gh}
.

The theory (
1
) is invariant under global
G=U_{L}(N_{f})\times U_{R}(N_{f})
“flavor” symmetry corresponding to independent unitary rotations of left- and right-handed chiral fermions. The matrix scalar field
H
is colorless but transform under
G
(see Table
1
).
In this paper we also consider a class of operators that breaks the flavor symmetry down to diagonal
U(N_{f})
. One can introduce independent couplings for these operators resulting in the following additional contribution to the Lagrangian (
1
)

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=-m_{\psi}\Tr(\bar{\psi}\psi)-\frac{h_{2}}{2}\left[\Tr(HH^{\dagger}H)+\mathrm{h.c.}\right]-\frac{h_{3}}{2}\left[\Tr(HH^{\dagger})\Tr(H)+\mathrm{h.c.}\right]

\displaystyle\equiv-m_{\psi}O_{1}-h_{2}O_{2}-h_{3}O_{3}=-\vec{\kappa}\cdot\vec{O}.

(2)

The choice of the
G
-breaking terms is dictated by the fact that the operator
O_{1}=\bar{\psi}\psi
, also considered in Ref.
Litim et al. 2023
, mixes under renormalization with the two operators coupled to
h_{2,3}
.

The model has four dimensionless couplings: gauge coupling
g
, the Yukawa
y
, and two quartic scalar couplings
u
and
v
. One usually introduces a set of rescaled couplings
’t Hooft 1974

\displaystyle\alpha_{g}=\frac{g^{2}N_{c}}{(4\pi^{2})},\quad\alpha_{y}=\frac{y^{2}N_{c}}{(4\pi^{2})},

\displaystyle\alpha_{u}=\frac{uN_{f}}{(4\pi^{2})},\quad\alpha_{v}=\frac{vN_{f}^{2}}{(4\pi^{2})}.

(3)

The latter has been done since we consider the Veneziano limit
Veneziano 1976
with
N_{f},N_{c}\to\infty
. One also introduces a parameter

\displaystyle\epsilon

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{N_{f}}{N_{c}}-\frac{11}{2}

(4)

that becomes continuous and may take any value between
(-\frac{11}{2},\infty)
.
The benefit of the Veneziano limit is that it allows systematic expansions in a small parameter.
In our work we suppose that

\displaystyle 0<\epsilon\leq 1,

(5)

and treat it as a small control parameter for perturbativity.

In order to study dimension-three operators in the Veneziano limit, we rescale the corresponding couplings and the operators as

\displaystyle m_{\psi}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=m_{\psi}\sqrt{N_{c}},

\displaystyle h^{\prime}_{2}

\displaystyle=h_{2}N_{f},

\displaystyle h^{\prime}_{3}

\displaystyle=h_{3}N_{f}^{2},

(6)

\displaystyle O^{\prime}_{1}

\displaystyle=O_{1}/\sqrt{N_{c}},

\displaystyle O^{\prime}_{2}

\displaystyle=O_{2}/N_{f},

\displaystyle O^{\prime}_{3}

\displaystyle=O_{3}/N_{f}^{2}.

(7)

This allows one to absorb all corrections with positive powers of
N_{c}
and
N_{f}
appearing in the beta functions for
\vec{\kappa}
into the rescaled couplings given in Eqs. (
3
) and (
6
). It is worth noticing that the parameters
m_{\phi}
,
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are rescaled in the same way as the dimensionless couplings
y
,
u
, and
v
, respectively.

III
Calculation methods

The beta functions and anomalous dimensions in the
\overline{\mathrm{MS}}
scheme can be computed by standard methods, so we omit full description of the techniques refereeing to appropriate literature. In this section, we only discuss peculiarities of the current calculation. Let us mention here that we do not rescale the couplings and operators according to Eqs. (
3
) and (
6
) in our explicit computation. The transition to the Veneziano-limit normalization is carried out at the final stage. Nevertheless, we present all our results in terms of
\alpha_{g,y,u,v}
and for the rescaled operators (
7
).

In order to compute the required RG functions, we rewrite the Lagrangian (
1
) in terms of real scalars
\phi^{a}
utilizing a decomposition (
a=1,\ldots,2N_{f}^{2}
)
Bednyakov and Pikelner 2021a

\displaystyle H=\phi^{a}T^{a},\qquad H^{\dagger}=\phi^{a}\bar{T}^{a},\qquad\bar{T}^{a}\equiv T^{a\dagger}

(8)

with
T^{a}
being complex
N_{f}\times N_{f}
matrices
1
1

1

One can also use a decomposition in terms of the identity matrix and
SU(N_{f})
generators as, e.g., in Refs.
Orlando et al. 2019
;
Antipin et al. 2022
.
normalized as

\displaystyle\Tr(T^{a}\bar{T}^{b})+\Tr(T^{b}\bar{T}^{a})=\delta^{ab}

(9)

and satisfying the following identities

\displaystyle T^{a}_{ij}T^{a}_{kl}=\bar{T}^{a}_{ij}\bar{T}^{a}_{kl}=0,\qquad T^{a}_{ij}\bar{T}^{a}_{kl}=\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}.

(10)

Such a decomposition gives rise to the Feynman rules (see Fig.
1
) for the vertices involving
\phi^{a}
.

\psi_{\alpha,L}^{i}
\psi_{\beta,R}^{j}
\phi^{a}

Figure 1:
Feynman rules for Yukawa and quartic vertices.
The indices
i,j=1,\ldots,N_{f}
count fermion generations (flavor), while
\alpha,\beta=1,\ldots,N_{c}
correspond to the
SU(N_{c})
gauge group. Flavor flows for left- and right-handed fermions are indicated.

In Fig.
1
we also indicate the flavor “flow” for the double-trace and single-trace scalar couplings that can be associated with the left (
L
) and right (
R
) chiral fermions. In the absence of
U_{L}(N_{f})\times U_{R}(N_{f})
breaking, the “left-” and “right-handed” flows are “conserved” separately.

We implemented the rules in the
DIANA

Tentyukov and Fleischer 2000
package based on
QGRAF

Nogueira 1993
and utilize
FORM

Vermaseren 1992
to deal with index contractions and to compute Feynman integrals via the
MATAD

Steinhauser 2001
code. In order to derive RG equations for
u
,
v
and
y
, we generate Green functions corresponding to radiative corrections for the tree-level Yukawa and quartic vertices up to three loops and extract local divergent terms by applying suitable projectors. Our explicit calculations heavily rely on well-known infrared rearrangement (IRR) trick
Vladimirov 1980
;
Misiak and Munz 1995
, which allows one to deal only with fully massive vacuum integrals.
The fermion-fermion-scalar interaction involves the
\gamma_{5}
-matrix, which requires special treatment in dimensional regularization (see, e.g., Ref.
Jegerlehner 2001
). In this paper, we restrict ourselves to the seminaive approach
Chetyrkin and Zoller 2012
;
Bednyakov et al. 2013
and by explicit computation we prove that potential ambiguities do not appear in the final result for the RG functions, thus, providing an independent cross-check of the results obtained in Ref.
Litim et al. 2023
.
As for the four-loop gauge-coupling beta function the
\gamma_{5}
-ambiguity can be fixed by means of Weyl consistency conditions
Jack and Osborn 2014
;
Antipin et al. 2013b
;
Poole and Thomsen 2019a
;
Poole and Thomsen 2019b
. Moreover, we do not carry out explicit computations here, but use the
RGBeta
code
Thomsen 2021
extended to 432-order in Refs.
Bednyakov and Pikelner 2021b
;
Davies et al. 2022
.

Let us now switch to the discussion of a family of dimension-three operators that includes
O_{1}=\bar{\psi}\psi
. In dimensionally regularized theory
2
2

2

Note the difference between the Veneziano parameter
\epsilon
and that of dimensional regularization
\varepsilon
.
(
d=4-2\varepsilon
) within the
\overline{\mathrm{MS}}
-scheme, we have a relation between bare and renormalized quantities

\displaystyle\vec{\kappa}_{R}\cdot[\vec{O}]_{R}

\displaystyle=\mu^{2\varepsilon}\cdot(\vec{\kappa})_{0}\cdot(\vec{O})_{0}=\mu^{2\varepsilon}\cdot(\vec{\kappa})_{R}\cdot\mathcal{Z}^{T}_{\kappa}\cdot\mathcal{Z}^{-1}_{O}\cdot[\vec{O}]_{R},

(11)

where all couplings in
\vec{\kappa}_{R}
, and all operators in
\vec{O}_{R}
have in our case the mass dimension one and three, respectively. The corresponding renormalization matrices
\mathcal{Z}_{\kappa}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{O}
are defined as

\displaystyle\vec{\kappa}_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}_{\kappa}(\mu,\alpha(\mu),\varepsilon)\cdot\vec{\kappa}_{R},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\kappa}(\mu,\alpha(\mu),\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&&\\
&\mu^{\varepsilon}&\\
&&\mu^{\varepsilon}\end{pmatrix}\cdot Z_{\kappa}(\alpha(\mu),\varepsilon),

(12)

\displaystyle[\vec{O}]_{R}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}_{O}(\mu,\alpha(\mu),\varepsilon)\cdot(\vec{O})_{0},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{O}(\mu,\alpha(\mu),\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=Z_{O}(\alpha(\mu),\varepsilon)\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\mu^{2\varepsilon}&&\\
&\mu^{3\varepsilon}&\\
&&\mu^{3\varepsilon}\end{pmatrix}.

(13)

The renormalization matrices involve poles in
\varepsilon
and depend on the renormalization scale
\mu
together with the running dimensionless couplings from (
1
) denoted collectively by
\alpha(\mu)
. They satisfy

\displaystyle\mu^{2\varepsilon}\mathcal{Z}_{\kappa}^{T}=\mathcal{Z}_{O}\Rightarrow Z^{T}_{\kappa}=Z_{O}

(14)

In what follows, we will routinely use
\mathcal{Z}
and
Z
to denote renormalization constants with and without explicit dependence on the renormalization scale
\mu
.
The diagonal matrices involving powers of
\mu^{\varepsilon}
account for mass dimensions of bare couplings and operators. We include them in the definition of
\mathcal{Z}
s for convenience, since we can write the beta functios of
\vec{\kappa}_{R}
in a compact form

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\ln\mu}\vec{\kappa}\equiv\dot{\vec{\kappa}}=\vec{\beta}_{\kappa}=\gamma_{\kappa}(\alpha)\cdot\vec{\kappa}\quad\gamma_{\kappa}(\alpha)=\dot{\mathcal{Z}}^{-1}_{\kappa}\cdot\mathcal{Z}_{\kappa}=-\mathcal{Z}^{-1}_{\kappa}\cdot\dot{\mathcal{Z}}_{\kappa},

(15)

and relate the matrix anomalous dimension
\gamma_{\kappa}
to the anomalous dimensions of the dimension-three operators

\displaystyle\gamma_{O}(\alpha)\equiv-\dot{\mathcal{Z}}_{O}\cdot\mathcal{Z}^{-1}_{O}=-2\varepsilon-\dot{\mathcal{Z}}^{T}_{\kappa}\cdot(\mathcal{Z}^{T})^{-1}_{\kappa}=-2\varepsilon+\gamma^{T}_{\kappa}(\alpha).

(16)

Both
\gamma_{O}(\alpha)
and
\gamma_{\kappa}(\alpha)
should be finite in the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
, which serves as a welcome check of the computation. As a consequence, in
d=4
we have

\displaystyle\gamma_{O}=\gamma_{\kappa}^{T}

(17)

This relation can be used in two ways. Given the beta functions of (a closed set of) the dimension-1 couplings, one can extract the matrix anomalous dimension for the corresponding operators. In case we know
\gamma_{O}
, it is possible to reconstruct the
\overline{\mathrm{MS}}
beta functions for the operator couplings, e.g., via

\displaystyle\beta_{m_{\psi}}

\displaystyle=m_{\psi}(\gamma_{O})_{11}+h_{2}(\gamma_{O})_{21}+h_{3}(\gamma_{O})_{31},

(18)

\displaystyle\beta_{h_{2}}

\displaystyle=m_{\psi}(\gamma_{O})_{12}+h_{2}(\gamma_{O})_{22}+h_{3}(\gamma_{O})_{32},

(19)

\displaystyle\beta_{h_{3}}

\displaystyle=m_{\psi}(\gamma_{O})_{13}+h_{2}(\gamma_{O})_{23}+h_{3}(\gamma_{O})_{33}.

(20)

In this paper we explicitly carry out the renormalization of dimension-three operators and compute
\gamma_{O}
up to three loops. In addition, we also cross-check our results at lower loops by adding (
2
) to the Litim-Sannino model (
1
) implemented in public computer codes
ARGES

Litim and Steudtner 2021
and
RGBeta
3
3

3

Correct treatment of dimension-1 couplings is implemented in RGBeta since version 1.1.5.

Thomsen 2021
and computing
\vec{\beta}_{\kappa}
.

\displaystyle=-i\delta_{\alpha\beta}\delta_{ij}(P_{L}+P_{R})\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\hskip 28.45274pt\hbox to47.21pt{\vbox to72.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 87.9152pt\lower-20.2132pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}+\text{5 perms.}]+\mathrm{h.c.}\}.

Figure 2:
Feynman rules for dimension-three operators.
All the operators break the flavor symmetry
G
by “flipping” the “chirality” of the flavor flow, which in the case of scalar operators we indicate by a box with a cross inside.

To find three-loop corrections to
Z_{O}
, we consider insertions of the operators
O_{1-3}
into one-particle irreducible (1PI) Green’s functions. The corresponding Feynman rules are given in Fig.
2
.
The renormalized 1PI Green functions are finite and are related to the bare ones via

\displaystyle\langle[O^{i}]_{R}\cdot\tilde{O}^{j}\rangle_{\text{1PI}}

\displaystyle=(Z_{O})_{ik}\cdot\langle O^{k}_{0}\cdot\tilde{O}^{j}_{0}\rangle_{\text{1PI}}\cdot Z_{j,ext}.

(21)

Here
\tilde{O}_{j}
is a product of external fields at different space-time points (either bare or renormalized) corresponding to the local operator
O_{j}
with a property that
\langle O^{i}\cdot\tilde{O}^{j}\rangle_{tree}\propto\delta^{ij}
. The factors
Z_{j,ext}
account for the external fields renormalization entering
\tilde{O}_{j}
, e.g.,
Z_{1,ext}=Z_{\psi}
,
Z_{2,ext}=Z_{3,ext}=Z_{H}^{3/2}
for
\psi_{0}=\sqrt{Z_{\psi}}\cdot\psi
, and

H_{0}=\sqrt{Z_{H}}\cdot H
. We determine the matrix elements of
(Z_{O})_{ij}
in the
\overline{\mathrm{MS}}
scheme order-by-order from the requirement that there are no
\varepsilon
poles in the rhs (
21
).

It is important to stress that if we ignore
O_{2,3}
(or the
h_{2,3}
couplings in the Lagrangian (
2
)), we can compute
(Z_{O})_{11}
and
(\gamma_{O})_{11}
(the latter is denoted by
\gamma_{m_{\psi}}
in Ref.
Litim et al. 2023
) by considering
\langle O_{1}(x)\psi(y)\bar{\psi}(z)\rangle_{\text{1PI}}
up to the two-loop order without hitting any difficulties. At three loops there are diagrams (see,e.g., Fig.
3
a), which require an insertion of the
O_{2}
operator as a counterterm (Fig.
3
b). Because of this, we are forced to include
O_{2}
in the game. The latter mixes with
O_{1}
already at the one-loop order
4
4

4

Speaking in other terms, the sole introduction of
m_{\psi}
in (
2
) will radiatively generate a
h_{2}
coupling at one-loop level (corresponding to a grey blob in Fig.
3
a).
. Moreover, starting from one loop, the
O_{3}
operator is needed to account for all the divergences appearing in four-point functions
\langle O_{2}\phi^{a}\phi^{b}\phi^{c}\rangle_{\text{1PI}}
.

O_{1}

O_{2}

(a)

(b)

Figure 3:
A three-loop diagram (a) with a
O_{1}=\bar{\psi}\psi
insertion that requires a two-loop counterterm due to
O_{2}
(b). The grey blob gives rise to a one-loop contribution to
(Z_{O})_{21}
.

We also compute the divergences of the two-point functions
\langle O_{i}(x)\phi^{a}(y)\rangle_{\text{1PI}}
for
i=1,2,3
and extract the mixing of
O_{i}
with
O_{4}=1/2(\partial^{2}\Tr(H)+\mathrm{h.c.})
, thus, modifying (
13
) as

\displaystyle[\vec{O}]_{R}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}_{O}\cdot(\vec{O})_{0}+\vec{\mathcal{Z}}(O_{4})_{0},

(22)

\displaystyle[O_{4}]_{R}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}_{H}^{-1/2}(O_{4})_{0}

(23)

This gives rise to a
4\times 4
mixing renormalization matrix
\mathcal{\bar{Z}}
entering

\displaystyle[O_{i}]_{R}

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{\bar{Z}}_{O})_{ij}(O_{j})_{0},\qquad\mathcal{\bar{Z}}_{O}=\bar{Z}_{O}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\mu^{2\epsilon}&&&\\
&\mu^{3\epsilon}&&\\
&&\mu^{3\epsilon}&\\
&&&\mu^{\epsilon}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\bar{\gamma}_{O}=-\dot{\mathcal{\bar{Z}}}_{O}\cdot\mathcal{\bar{Z}}_{O}^{-1}

(24)

where
i=1,\ldots,4
and
\bar{Z}_{O}
is schematically depicted in Fig.
4
. If we take into account the equations of motion (EOM) for the bare operators

\displaystyle(O_{4})_{0}+\vec{\Lambda}_{0}\cdot(\vec{O})_{0}=0,\qquad\vec{\Lambda}_{0}=\left\{y_{0}/2,2u_{0},2v_{0}\right\}\equiv\left\{\mu^{\varepsilon}Z_{y}(y/2),\mu^{2\varepsilon}Z_{u}(2u),\mu^{2\varepsilon}Z_{v}(2v)\right\}

(25)

where
Z_{y,u,v}
are renormalization constants for the corresponding couplings, and express
O_{4}
as a linear combination of
\vec{O}
, we obtain another
3\times 3
matrix

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{O}\to\mathcal{\tilde{Z}}_{O}\equiv\mathcal{Z}_{O}-\vec{\mathcal{Z}}\otimes\vec{\Lambda}_{0},

(26)

schematically presented in Fig.
5
. The corresponding anomalous dimension is given by

\displaystyle\tilde{\gamma}_{O}\equiv-\dot{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}}_{O}\cdot\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{-1}_{O}

(27)

and is different from
\gamma_{O}
(
16
). At a fixed point,
\tilde{\gamma}^{*}_{O}
(
\gamma^{*}_{O}
) has a eigenvalue
\gamma^{*}_{H}
(
-\gamma^{*}_{H}
). The other two eigenvalues of
\gamma^{*}_{O}
coincide with those of
\tilde{\gamma}^{*}_{O}
.
This can be interpreted as the fact that only two of the considered dimension-three eigenoperators are independent, while the remaining one is a descendent of
\Tr(H)+\mathrm{h.c.}

\displaystyle\bar{Z}_{O}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}Z_{O}&\vec{Z}\\
\vec{0}&Z^{-1/2}_{H}\end{pmatrix}\equiv\mathcal{KR}^{\prime}\begin{pmatrix}\hbox to61.94pt{\vbox to43.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 104.1135pt\lower-7.61319pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{

Figure 4:
One-particle-irreducible Green functions used to extract matrix elements of
Z_{O}
. The factor
Z_{H}^{-1/2}
enters the matrix
\bar{Z}_{O}
due to
[O_{4}]_{R}=Z_{H}^{-1/2}(O_{4})_{0}
.

Before discussing results, let us note how our approach differs from that of Ref.
Litim et al. 2023
. The authors of Ref.
Litim et al. 2023
utilized the same IRR technique and the seminaive treatment of
\gamma_{5}
in their three-loop calculations. However, instead of
DIANA
and
MATAD
, they used a private framework to deal with Feynman diagrams generated by
QGRAF
. In our study we
do not
rely on the dummy-field method (see, e.g., Ref.
Schienbein et al. 2019
and references therein) to derive the beta functions of dimensional couplings, but extract them from the Green’s functions with insertions of the corresponding operators.
Moreover, the authors of Ref.
Litim et al. 2023
considered only the
O_{1}=\bar{\psi}\psi
operator and did not account for possible mixing with
O_{2,3}
. Because of this, the anomalous dimension
5
5

5

Corresponding to
(\gamma_{O})_{11}=(\gamma_{\kappa})_{11}
in our notation.
denoted by
\gamma_{m_{\psi}}
is
not
an eigenvalue of the corresponding matrix and, if computed at a fixed point, does
not
represent a correction to canonical scaling.

In what follows, we present all our results in terms of rescaled couplings and for the rescaled operators
O^{\prime}
(
7
).
All expressions are available in computer-readable form as Supplemental
Material.

IV
Results

In this section, we summarize our results for
\beta
-functions for (in)finite
N_{c}
and anomalous dimensions. Moreover, we determine fixed points and universal scaling dimensions up to the third nontrivial order in the Veneziano parameter also for both cases.

Table 2:
The model beta-functions at the fixed point as series in
\epsilon
. Cancellations between contributions from different loop orders are presented up to
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{5})
for
\beta_{g}
and up to
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{4})
for
\beta_{y,u,v}
. Higher-order terms marked by the red color are not reliably
calculated and are not summed up to zero, but (the sum) can be used to estimate the size of typical high-order contribution.

IV.0.1
Fixed points

As it is well known in perturbation theory the
\beta
-functions can be given as

\displaystyle\beta_{x}\equiv\frac{d\alpha_{x}}{d\ln\mu}=\beta_{x}^{(1)}+\beta_{x}^{(2)}+\beta_{x}^{(3)}+...,

(28)

where
\beta_{x}^{(n)}
denotes the
n
-th loop contribution, and
x=\{g,y,u,v\}
. In Ref.
Litim et al. 2023
they were found for the first time and provided both in the Veneziano limit and with finite-
N_{c}
corrections. We recomputed them independently and provide the lengthy expressions in Appendix
A.1
and as Supplemental
Material.

Using the 433-order
\beta
functions and solving
\beta_{i}(\alpha_{j}^{*})=0
systematically, we determine interacting fixed points up to complete third order in the small parameter
\epsilon
. We expressed fixed points as a series expansion

\displaystyle\alpha_{x}^{*}

\displaystyle=c_{x}^{(1)}f_{x}^{(1)}(N_{c})\epsilon+c_{x}^{(2)}f_{x}^{(2)}(N_{c})\epsilon^{2}+c_{x}^{(3)}f_{x}^{(3)}(N_{c})\epsilon^{3}+O(\epsilon^{4}),

(29)

where
x=\{g,y,u,v\}
.
We have 6 possible solutions with fixed points, however, following Refs.
Litim and Sannino 2014
;
Bond and Litim 2018
;
Bond et al. 2021
, we choose a fully interacting UV fixed point (
\alpha_{g,y,u,v}^{*}\neq 0
) that exhibits asymptotically safe behavior.

The coefficients
c_{x}^{(i)}
of series expansion can be found by solving the beta functions in this limit order by order for the stationary point. We recomputed the exact expressions for
c_{x}^{(i)}
and found agreement with Ref.
Litim et al. 2023

\displaystyle c_{g}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{26}{57},\qquad c_{g}^{(2)}=-\frac{23\left(13068\sqrt{23}-75245\right)}{370386},

(30)

\displaystyle c_{g}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(5025189312\zeta_{3}+353747709269-71703657432\sqrt{23}\right)}{2406768228},

(31)

\displaystyle c_{y}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{19},\qquad c_{y}^{(2)}=\frac{\left(43549-6900\sqrt{23}\right)}{20577},

(32)

\displaystyle c_{y}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(29734848\zeta_{3}+2893213181-580847448\sqrt{23}\right)}{44569782},

(33)

\displaystyle c_{u}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{23}-1}{19},\qquad c_{u}^{(2)}=\frac{365825\sqrt{23}-1476577}{631028},

(34)

\displaystyle c_{u}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{5173524931447\sqrt{23}-24197965967251}{282928976136}-\frac{416\left(\sqrt{23}-12\right)\zeta_{3}}{6859}\right),

(35)

\displaystyle c_{v}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{2\left(10+3\sqrt{23}\right)}-2\sqrt{23}}{19},

(36)

\displaystyle c_{v}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\frac{268229312-68836310\sqrt{23}+46652027\sqrt{2\left(10+3\sqrt{23}\right)}-9153184\sqrt{46\left(10+3\sqrt{23}\right)}}{67519996},

(37)

\displaystyle c_{v}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=+\frac{1}{3239253847781064}\left[498710025259776\zeta_{3}-105786975055104\sqrt{23}\zeta_{3}\right.{}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\left.+6742118880147456\sqrt{2\left(10+3\sqrt{23}\right)}\zeta_{3}-1374312631206528\sqrt{46\left(10+3\sqrt{23}\right)}\zeta_{3}\right.{}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\left.+479791813615522776-103064713697904086\sqrt{23}+74641138195038841\sqrt{2\left(10+3\sqrt{23}\right)}\right.{}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\left.-15585870376520334\sqrt{46\left(10+3\sqrt{23}\right)}\right]

(38)

For convenience, we provide the numerical results in the Veneziano limit
Litim et al. 2023

\displaystyle\alpha_{g}^{*}

\displaystyle=0.456\epsilon+0.781\epsilon^{2}+6.610\epsilon^{3}{\color[rgb]{1,0.3008,0.4023}+24.137}\epsilon^{4},

(39)

\displaystyle\alpha_{y}^{*}

\displaystyle=0.211\epsilon+0.508\epsilon^{2}+3.322\epsilon^{3}{\color[rgb]{1,0.3008,0.4023}+15.212}\epsilon^{4},

(40)

\displaystyle\alpha_{u}^{*}

\displaystyle=0.200\epsilon+0.440\epsilon^{2}+2.693\epsilon^{3}{\color[rgb]{1,0.3008,0.4023}+12.119}\epsilon^{4},

(41)

\displaystyle-\alpha_{v}^{*}

\displaystyle=0.137\epsilon+0.632\epsilon^{2}+4.313\epsilon^{3}{\color[rgb]{1,0.3008,0.4023}+24.147}\epsilon^{4},

(42)

where we also include subleading terms
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{4})
terms that will be modified when the
544
-result will be available
Litim et al. 2023
.
In addition, Table
2
shows various
\beta^{(l)}_{x}
evaluated at the fixed point
(\alpha_{g}^{*},\alpha_{y}^{*},\alpha_{u}^{*},\alpha_{v}^{*})
given above. One sees the typical size of the coefficients that cancel at each order of
\epsilon
-expansion up to
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{5})
in
\beta_{g}
, and up to
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{4})
in other beta functions. Subleading terms that do not add up to zero due to missing high-order corrections are also indicated.

The functions
f_{x}^{(i)}
first introduced in Ref.
Bond et al. 2021
capture the dependence on
N_{c}
and can be computed in the same manner as the
c_{x}^{(i)}
. It should be noted that
\lim_{N_{c}\to\infty}f_{x}^{(i)}\equiv 1
, which means that the FPs found for in(finite) cases are connected continuously. Therefore, for the infinite-
N_{c}
case we have only
c_{x}^{(i)}
, which are exact numbers. However, the full form of
f_{x}^{(i)}
is complicated
6
6

6

Available by demand from the authors.
. Because of this, following the ideas of
Litim and Steudtner 2021
, we fitted all the finite-
N_{c}
corrections in the range
N_{c}\in[3,100]
as ratios of two second-order polynomial up to
\epsilon^{3}
. The results can be found in Appendix
B
.
Then, we carried out another fit when ratios of two fourth-order polynomials were considered and obtained the following expressions:

\displaystyle f_{g}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{c}^{2}}{N_{c}^{2}-\frac{110}{19}},\qquad f_{g}^{(2)}=\frac{N_{c}^{4}-0.534N_{c}^{2}+2.485}{N_{c}^{4}-13.106N_{c}^{2}+43.594},\qquad f_{g}^{(3)}=\frac{N_{c}^{4}+8.103N_{c}^{2}+40.270}{N_{c}^{4}-15.278N_{c}^{2}+59.731},

(43)

\displaystyle f_{y}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{c}^{2}-1}{N_{c}^{2}-\frac{110}{19}},\qquad f_{y}^{(2)}=\frac{N_{c}^{4}-0.976N_{c}^{2}+1.185}{N_{c}^{4}-12.691N_{c}^{2}+40.681},\qquad f_{y}^{(3)}=\frac{N_{c}^{4}+6.520N_{c}^{2}+32.220}{N_{c}^{4}-15.226N_{c}^{2}+59.311},

(44)

\displaystyle f_{u}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{c}^{4}-1.047N_{c}^{2}+0.047}{N_{c}^{4}-5.863N_{c}^{2}+0.428},\qquad f_{u}^{(2)}=\frac{N_{c}^{4}-1.049N_{c}^{2}+1.545}{N_{c}^{4}-12.779N_{c}^{2}+41.292},\qquad f_{u}^{(3)}=\frac{N_{c}^{4}+7.239N_{c}^{2}+35.870}{N_{c}^{4}-15.284N_{c}^{2}+59.770},

(45)

\displaystyle f_{v}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{c}^{4}-1.029N_{c}^{2}+0.029}{N_{c}^{4}-5.878N_{c}^{2}+0.511},\qquad f_{v}^{(2)}=\frac{N_{c}^{4}-1.718N_{c}^{2}+1.0112}{N_{c}^{4}-12.330N_{c}^{2}+38.240},\qquad f_{v}^{(3)}=\frac{N_{c}^{4}+4.258N_{c}^{2}+19.300}{N_{c}^{4}-14.946N_{c}^{2}+56.311}.

(46)

which turn out to be more accurate. .
The maximal value of the mean squared error (MSE) for the
f_{x}^{(i)}
with our fourth (second) order polynomial fits in each order of
\epsilon
is as follows:
10^{-12}
(
4\cdot 10^{-11}
),
10^{-9}
(
8\cdot 10^{-5}
),
8\cdot 10^{-5}
(
4\cdot 10^{-3}
).
While the fit with the second-order polynomials seems to provide good approximation we restrict ourselves to a more precise four-order result.

At the end, we carry our numerical comparison of the two types of fits and exact results. The green line in Fig.
6
corresponds to the second order fit (see Appendix
B
), and purple line is our fourth order fit (
43
),(
44
),(
45
),(
46
). One can see that the green line “misses” exact points in Fig.
6
in the presented range of
N_{c}
, while the purple line goes through the dots.

The dependence of couplings from the number of colors
N_{c}
for fixed

). The black dots correspond to exact values. It should be noted that we omit the point

, since it lies higher in the FP’s scale, therefore the difference between the fits is not clearly visible.

IV.0.2
Critical exponents

Let us study the universal critical exponents. The latter can be obtained as the eigenvalues of the stability matrix

\displaystyle M_{ij}=\frac{\partial\beta_{i}}{\partial\alpha_{j}}|_{\alpha=\alpha^{*}},

(47)

which again we expand as a power series

\displaystyle\theta_{j}=c_{\theta_{j}}^{(1)}f_{\theta_{j}}^{(1)}\epsilon+c_{\theta_{j}}^{(2)}f_{\theta_{j}}^{(2)}\epsilon^{2}+c_{\theta_{j}}^{(3)}f_{\theta_{j}}^{(3)}\epsilon^{3}+...

(48)

and find perfect agreement with
Litim et al. 2023
(note that for
\theta_{1}
expansion starts at
\epsilon^{2}
so
c^{(1)}_{\theta_{1}}=0
):

\displaystyle c^{(2)}_{\theta_{1}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{104}{171},\qquad c^{(3)}_{\theta_{1}}=\frac{2296}{3249},\qquad c^{(4)}_{\theta_{1}}=\frac{\left(43551640704\zeta_{3}+1405590649319-281341851912\sqrt{23}\right)}{15643993482},

(49)

\displaystyle c^{(1)}_{\theta_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{52}{19},\quad c^{(2)}_{\theta_{2}}=\frac{\left(136601719-22783308\sqrt{23}\right)}{4094823},\quad c^{(3)}_{\theta_{2}}=\frac{5\left(547695099865475491-111718308712462080\sqrt{23}\right)}{2692813775855538},

(50)

\displaystyle c^{(1)}_{\theta_{3}}

\displaystyle=\frac{8}{19}\sqrt{20+6\sqrt{23}},\qquad c^{(2)}_{\theta_{3}}=\frac{2\left(45155739-9153184\sqrt{23}\right)\sqrt{20+6\sqrt{23}}}{16879999},

(51)

\displaystyle c^{(3)}_{\theta_{3}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{20+6\sqrt{23}}\left(-918044509824\left(1497\sqrt{23}-7558\right)\zeta_{3}+73205713038142585-15289473238519518\sqrt{23}\right)}{404906730972633},

(52)

\displaystyle c^{(1)}_{\theta_{4}}

\displaystyle=\frac{16\sqrt{23}}{19},\qquad c^{(2)}_{\theta_{4}}=\frac{4\left(68248487\sqrt{23}-255832864\right)}{31393643},

(53)

\displaystyle c^{(3)}_{\theta_{4}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\left(37418532792608300581\sqrt{23}-174067504271892880236\right)}{278706225801048183}.

(54)

Numerical evaluation of these coefficients gives in the Veneziano limit
Litim et al. 2023

\displaystyle-\theta_{1}

\displaystyle=0.608\epsilon^{2}-0.707\epsilon^{3}-6.947\epsilon^{4}{\color[rgb]{1,0.3008,0.4023}-4.825\epsilon^{5}},

(55)

\displaystyle\theta_{2}

\displaystyle=2.737\epsilon+6.676\epsilon^{2}+22.120\epsilon^{3}{\color[rgb]{1,0.3008,0.4023}+102.55\epsilon^{4}},

(56)

\displaystyle\theta_{3}

\displaystyle=2.941\epsilon+1.041\epsilon^{2}+5.137\epsilon^{3}{\color[rgb]{1,0.3008,0.4023}-62.340\epsilon^{4}},

(57)

\displaystyle\theta_{4}

\displaystyle=4.039\epsilon+9.107\epsilon^{2}+38.646\epsilon^{3}{\color[rgb]{1,0.3008,0.4023}+87.016\epsilon^{4}}

(58)

and for not too large
\epsilon
(see below) there is only one relevant direction corresponding to
\theta_{1}<0
giving rise to an asymptotically-safe scenario. In Eq. (
58
) we again highlight terms that are not determined precisely in the 433 approximation and will be modified by high-order terms.

Finite-
N_{c}
corrections are incorporated in the factors, which we approximate as

IV.0.3
Other anomalous dimensions

In this subsection we provide results for the scalar and fermion anomalous dimensions. In addition, we consider the operator
\Tr(H^{\dagger}H)
coupled to
m^{2}
in (
1
) together with the anomalous dimensions corresponding to dimension-three eigenoperators discussed earlier.
The full expressions for anomalous dimensions beyond the Veneziano limit can be found in Appendix
A.3
and are also available in the Supplemental Material.

At the fixed point the anomalous dimensions are given as a series expansion in
\epsilon
.
In the Veneziano limit we have gauge-independent coefficients

\displaystyle c_{H}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{19},

\displaystyle c_{H}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\frac{14567}{6859}-\frac{2376\sqrt{23}}{6859},

\displaystyle c_{H}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\frac{8816623159}{133709346}-\frac{753675598}{2476099\sqrt{23}},

(63)

for the scalar fields, and

\displaystyle c_{\psi}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{11}{19},\qquad c_{\psi}^{(2)}=\frac{3738501-683100\sqrt{23}}{740772},

\displaystyle c_{\psi}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2879380764\zeta(3)+780746553081-158608932408\sqrt{23}}{4813536456},

(64)

which is valid in the Landau gauge
\xi=\xi^{*}=0
(see also Ref.
Litim et al. 2023
).
The gauge-independent result for
m^{2}
can be cast into

\displaystyle c_{m^{2}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{19}\sqrt{2(10+3\sqrt{23})},\qquad c_{m^{2}}^{(2)}=\frac{\left(45155739-9153184\sqrt{23}\right)\sqrt{20+6\sqrt{23}}}{16879999},

\displaystyle c_{m^{2}}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{20+6\sqrt{23}}\left(-918044509824\left(1497\sqrt{23}-7558\right)\zeta(3)+73205713038142585-15289473238519518\sqrt{23}\right)}{809813461945266}.

(65)

while gluon (
G
) and ghost (
c
) field anomalous dimensions in the Landau gauge
8
8

8

The result
\gamma_{G}=-2\gamma_{c}
in the Landau gauge is the consequence of the relation for the renormalization constant for gluon-ghost-ghost vertex
Z_{Gcc}=Z_{g}\cdot Z_{G}^{1/2}\cdot Z_{c}
that leads to
\gamma_{Gcc}=(\gamma_{G}+2\gamma_{c})-\beta_{\alpha_{g}}/(2\alpha_{g})
. Since
\beta_{\alpha_{g}}(\alpha^{*})=0
and in the Landau gauge
Z_{Gcc}=1
, we have
\gamma_{Gcc}=0
and
\gamma_{G}+2\gamma_{c}=0
.
are given as

\displaystyle c_{G}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=-2c_{c}^{(1)}=\frac{13}{19},\qquad c_{G}^{(2)}=-2c_{c}^{(2)}=\frac{188725-33396\sqrt{23}}{27436},

\displaystyle c_{G}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=-2c_{c}^{(3)}=\frac{288694588\zeta(3)+38849548925-7884584928\sqrt{23}}{178279128}.

(66)

Substituting the coefficients into the power series, we have in the Veneziano limit and (for
\xi=0
)
Litim et al. 2023

\displaystyle\gamma_{H}

\displaystyle=0.2105\epsilon+0.4625\epsilon^{2}+2.4711\epsilon^{3},

(67)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\psi}

\displaystyle=0.5789\epsilon+0.6243\epsilon^{2}+4.8916\epsilon^{3},

(68)

\displaystyle\gamma_{m^{2}}

\displaystyle=1.4703\epsilon+0.5207\epsilon^{2}+2.5684\epsilon^{3},

(69)

\displaystyle\gamma_{G}

\displaystyle=-2\gamma_{c}=0.6842\epsilon+1.0411\epsilon^{2}+7.7599\epsilon^{3}.

(70)

The factors that account for the finite-
N_{c}
corrections are again approximated as

\displaystyle f_{H}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{c}^{2}-1}{N_{c}^{2}-\frac{110}{19}},

(72)

\displaystyle f_{m^{2}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{c}^{4}-2.678N_{c}^{2}+1.677}{N_{c}^{4}-7.468N_{c}^{2}+9.715},

\displaystyle f_{m^{2}}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{c}^{4}+9.603N_{c}^{2}+11.895}{N_{c}^{4}-13.542N_{c}^{2}+46.829},

\displaystyle f_{m^{2}}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{c}^{4}+47.739N_{c}^{2}+253.184}{N_{c}^{4}-15.868N_{c}^{2}+64.530},

(73)

\displaystyle f_{G(c)}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{c}^{2}}{N_{c}^{2}-\frac{110}{19}},

\displaystyle f_{G(c)}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{c}^{4}-0.522N_{c}^{2}+3.152}{N_{c}^{4}-13.217N_{c}^{2}+44.395},

\displaystyle f_{G(c)}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{c}^{4}+9.086N_{c}^{2}+48.136}{N_{c}^{4}-15.440N_{c}^{2}+61.028}.

(74)

where the maximal MSE is no worse than
3\cdot 10^{-7}
in comparison to
10^{-1}
for the approximations given in Eq. (
163
).

Finally, the eigenvalues for the dimension-three operator

\displaystyle\gamma_{j}

V
Conformal window

In this section we investigate the size of the UV conformal window for the asymptotically safe theory with action equation (
1
) using perturbation theory.

V.1
Constraints on the UV conformal window

We can find the UV conformal window directly from the expressions for fixed points and scaling exponents given in the previous sections. In the case when we retain only first three non-vanishing powers of
\epsilon
, we will call the bound on
\epsilon
strict.
The reason for this is that the higher-order (“subleading”) coefficients in the power expansion (
29
),(
48
) are not (yet) accurately determined due to the absence of higher loop terms in
\beta
-functions. This scheme is dictated first by the following constraints

•

perturbativity for couplings
0<|\alpha^{*}|\lesssim 1

Weinberg 1979
;

•

vacuum stability
\alpha_{u}^{*}>0
and
\alpha_{u}^{*}+\alpha_{v}^{*}>0

Paterson 1981
;

•

no fixed point merger
Bond et al. 2021
(the collision of the UV fixed point with an IR fixed point corresponds to
\theta=0
).

The second strategy employs the approximation, where we retain subleading terms in
\epsilon
, so we refer to its bounds as subleading. There we also take into account all the above mentioned constraints from couplings, vacuum stability and critical exponents.

V.2
Investigation of the UV conformal window

The UV conformal window can be investigated using the above mentioned constraints. To do so, we first can equate the perturbative expressions for FP couplings to unity, (
\alpha_{u}^{*}+\alpha_{v}^{*}
) and scaling exponents to zero and choose the smallest positive solution for
\epsilon
. Second, we can find the Padé approximants for these constraints and make the same manipulations. We represent our results as
\alpha^{*}=\epsilon P_{ij}
and
\theta=\epsilon^{2}P_{ij}
, where
P_{ij}
are Padé approximants and
i+j=2(3)
for the strict (subleading) case. However, we cannot confidently trust the obtained results, because these approximants contain non-physical poles. Nevertheless, they give tighter constraints on the conformal window and we provide all approximants that can be constructed from available series together with the corresponding bounds.

•

From perturbative expansion of couplings (
39
)-(
42
), we note that the tightest bound on
\epsilon_{strict}
arises from the gauge coupling.
First, equating the gauge coupling to unity, we can find
\epsilon_{strict}
and
\epsilon_{subl}

\displaystyle\epsilon_{strict}\approx 0.457,\qquad\epsilon_{subl}\approx 0.363.

(88)

Second, we can construct the Padé approximants

\displaystyle P_{11}^{\alpha_{g}^{*}}

\displaystyle=\frac{0.456-3.081\epsilon}{1-8.467\epsilon},

(89)

\displaystyle P_{12}^{\alpha_{g}^{*}}

\displaystyle=\frac{0.456-0.328\epsilon}{1-2.431\epsilon-10.331\epsilon^{2}},\qquad P_{21}^{\alpha_{g}^{*}}=\frac{0.456-0.885\epsilon+3.760\epsilon^{2}}{1-3.651\epsilon}

where the first line correspond to the strict case, the second to subleading, and find the bounds

\displaystyle\epsilon_{strict_{11}}

\displaystyle\approx 0.117,\qquad\epsilon_{subl_{12}}

\displaystyle\approx 0.203,\qquad\epsilon_{subl_{21}}\approx 0.243.

(90)

•

In the same manner we can investigate the bounds arising from the vacuum stability condition
Paterson 1981
:

\displaystyle\epsilon_{strict}\approx 0.146,\qquad\epsilon_{subl}\approx 0.116.

(91)

To provide more stronger constrains for
\epsilon
, we use the Padé approximants:

\displaystyle P_{11}^{\alpha_{u}^{*}+\alpha_{v}^{*}}

\displaystyle=\frac{0.0625-0.719\epsilon}{1-8.438\epsilon},

(92)

\displaystyle P_{12}^{\alpha_{u}^{*}+\alpha_{v}^{*}}

\displaystyle=\frac{0.625-0.673\epsilon}{1-7.695\epsilon+2.281\epsilon^{2}},\qquad P_{21}^{\alpha_{u}^{*}+\alpha_{v}^{*}}=\frac{0.625-0.656\epsilon-0.194\epsilon^{2}}{1-7.425\epsilon}.

The UV conformal window in this case is constrained as

\displaystyle\epsilon_{strict_{11}}

\displaystyle\approx 0.087,\qquad\epsilon_{subl_{12}}

\displaystyle\approx 0.09287,\qquad\epsilon_{subl_{21}}\approx 0.09272.

(93)

•

After the calculation of scaling exponents (
58
),
we notice the behavior of
\theta_{2,3,4}
with same-sign corrections at every order. However, the sign of leading
\epsilon^{2}
coefficient for the relevant scaling exponent
\theta_{1}
differs from other loop terms, which is the indication of possible FP merger.
Thus, we can extract the constraints from the relevant scaling exponent, solving
\theta_{1}=0
:

\displaystyle\epsilon_{strict}\approx 0.249,\qquad\epsilon_{subl}\approx 0.234.

(94)

And using the Padé approximation:

\displaystyle P_{11}^{\theta_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{0.608-6.681\epsilon}{1-9.826\epsilon},

(95)

\displaystyle P_{12}^{\theta_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{0.608-1.717\epsilon}{1-0.661\epsilon+9.495\epsilon^{2}},\qquad P_{21}^{\theta_{1}}=\frac{0.608-1.129\epsilon-6.455\epsilon^{2}}{1-0.695\epsilon},

we get

\displaystyle\epsilon_{strict_{11}}

\displaystyle\approx 0.091,\qquad\epsilon_{subl_{12}}

\displaystyle\approx 0.354,\qquad\epsilon_{subl_{21}}\approx 0.232.

(96)

It should be noted, that at 433 order we have only one fixed point merger. However, if the subleading tendency continues in the 544 order, it will lead to the additional
\theta_{3}
merger (
57
), which we do not study in this paper.

Moreover, we illustrate our results in the Fig.
7
.

\epsilon_{strict,subl}
bounds for 433 order (full lines) (
88
),(
91
),(
94
) and their tighter Padé ressummations (dashed lines) (
90
),(

),(

). The 211 and 322 orders also illustrated.

Here we show up the bounds for strict and subleading approaches (full lines) (

),(

),(

) and their Padé approximants (dashed lines) that provide tightest constraints(

),(

),(

) at 433 order. In addition, we add 211 and 322 orders for comparison.
From these figures and from (

),(

),(

),(

),(

), and (
96
), we can deduce that the
\epsilon_{subl}
bound is systematically tighter than the
\epsilon_{strict}
bound, which arise from the last summands (
39
), (
55
). The same situation was obtained at 322 order, see Ref.
Bond et al. 2018
.

At the end, we demonstrate the size of the UV conformal window in Fig.
8
. The left panel includes
\epsilon_{strict}
bounds arising from vacuum stability (
91
),(
93
). For comparison we have also indicated the previous bound at 322 order
Bond et al. 2018
.
The right panel illustrates the boundaries for finite values of
N_{c}
. From this picture we can see that all constraints (
88
),(
91
),(
94
) share roughly the same rate of convergence. In addition, it is clear, that finite-
N_{c}
corrections contract the conformal window (full lines) in comparison to infinite results (dashed lines).

Left panel: conformal window with asymptotic safety (yellow band), also showing regimes with asymptotic freedom (green) and effective theories (grey). Dots indicate the first few integer solutions (
97
) at 433 order. Moreover, this panel contains the new upper bound on
\epsilon
at 433 order (
91
), and the tighter Padé approximant bound (dashed line) (

). Previous upper bounds at 322
Bond et al. 2018
order also indicated. The right panel compares different schemes (
88
),(

),(

) as given by
\epsilon_{strict}
. The dashed lines represent the asymptotic value; the full lines represent the upper boundary for the
\epsilon
as functions of
N_{c}
.

Finally, using the tightest bound on the conformal window which is given by the vacuum instability
\epsilon_{strict}\approx 0.146
(
91
), we obtain the smallest pair of integer values for (
N_{c}
,
N_{f}
) compatible with asymptotic safety, which are indicated by black dots in Fig.
8
. The first few integer solutions are

\displaystyle(N_{c},N_{f})=(5,28),(7,39),(8,45),(9,50),(10,56),(11,61),(11,62),(12,67)...

(97)

V.3
(De)stabilizing fluctuations

Following the ideas of Refs.
Bond et al. 2018
;
Bond et al. 2021
, let us consider into which direction the higher loop corrections shift the beta functions. To do this we substitute the fixed points to order
O(\epsilon)^{3}
back to the beta functions and take series expansion in
\epsilon
up to the first non-vanishing order. Keeping only highest available terms in RG functions, we obtain
(c.f., also Table
2
)

\displaystyle\beta_{g}^{(4)}|_{322}

\displaystyle=-4.154\left(\frac{55.257+N_{c}^{2}}{-8.708+N_{c}^{2}}\right)\epsilon^{5},

(98)

\displaystyle\beta_{y}^{(3)}|_{322}

\displaystyle=1.263\left(\frac{33.923+N_{c}^{2}}{-8.442+N_{c}^{2}}\right)\epsilon^{4},

\displaystyle\beta_{u}^{(3)}|_{322}

\displaystyle=-0.252\left(\frac{19.213+N_{c}^{2}}{-8.304+N_{c}^{2}}\right)\epsilon^{4},

\displaystyle\beta_{v}^{(3)}|_{322}

\displaystyle=0.213\left(\frac{83.582+N_{c}^{2}}{-8.514+N_{c}^{2}}\right)\epsilon^{4}.

Negative shifts to the beta functions (
\Delta\beta<0
) are supposed to stabilize the UV fixed point
Bond et al. 2018
;
Bond et al. 2021
, while
\Delta\beta>0
, conversely, shift the zero towards larger values, which could even destabilize it. Thus, the obtained leading shifts for finite
N_{c}
gives us a qualitative picture of the trend from higher-order loop contributions. It should be noted that these shifts have changed their signs compared to the previous results obtained in Refs.
Bond et al. 2018
;
Bond et al. 2021
.

In the same manner, we insert the fixed points up to
\epsilon^{3}
to the full available beta functions and find the subleading shifts for finite
N_{c}
(see Table
2
with the Veneziano limit):

\displaystyle\beta_{g}|_{433}

\displaystyle=65.931\left(\frac{183.114+N_{c}^{2}}{-8.948+N_{c}^{2}}\right)\epsilon^{6},

(99)

\displaystyle\beta_{y}|_{433}

\displaystyle=-11.143\left(\frac{75.486+N_{c}^{2}}{-8.946+N_{c}^{2}}\right)\epsilon^{5},

\displaystyle\beta_{u}|_{433}

\displaystyle=9.354\left(\frac{64.415+N_{c}^{2}}{-8.877+N_{c}^{2}}\right)\epsilon^{5},

\displaystyle\beta_{v}|_{433}

\displaystyle=47.863\left(\frac{51.740+N_{c}^{2}}{-7.993+N_{c}^{2}}\right)\epsilon^{5}.

We see that the results for
\beta_{g,y,u}
change signs as compared to (
98
). However, they retained their behavior as in the case of Refs.
Bond et al. 2018
;
Bond et al. 2021
. Therefore, we can expect, that in the 544 order we will obtain similar results.

VI
Conclusion

The availability of interacting UV fixed points in particle physics presents numerous prospects for constructing models, see, e.g., Ref.
Bond et al. 2017
. However, comprehending the size of the corresponding conformal window is equally crucial for any real-world applications. In this paper we investigated the Litim-Sannino model with action (
1
) at the 433 order. Extending the findings of
Litim and Sannino 2014
;
Bond et al. 2018
;
Bond et al. 2021
and confirming the results of
Litim et al. 2023
, we have performed a full search for interacting fixed points and computed scaling exponents and anomalous dimensions up to third order in the small parameter
\epsilon
(
4
). It was also noted that their full expressions for finite
N_{c}
are complicated, therefore, we performed fourth(second) order fits and provide the corresponding approximate expressions together. By comparing means square errors, we conclude that the ratio of fourth-order polynomials provide better approximation in certain cases; see, e.g., Fig.
6
.

Moreover, we studied the size of the conformal window imposing conditions on the fixed point values of the couplings and scaling exponents. We used different approximation orders and estimate the effect of subleading corrections. We compared various restrictions coming from the perturbativity of the strong coupling (
88
), vacuum instability (
91
) and possible fixed point merger (
94
). Despite their qualitatively different origins, constraints are quantitatively similar, with vacuum stability offering the tightest bound (
91
). In addition, following Ref.
Litim et al. 2023
we tried to resum the
\epsilon
series by means of Padé approximation and find that it gives even stronger constraints (
90
),(
93
),(
96
). However, the presence of non-physical poles in the approximants used to derive the bounds undermines our confidence in their accuracy. Perhaps, this should be explored using other types of approximations and when high-order contributions in the 544-scheme will be available. We summarize our results in Fig.
8
, where we illustrated the asymptotic safety regime together with the size of the UV conformal window. At the end, the asymptotically safe quantum field theories, which lie within the allowed conformal window were also demonstrated in Eq.(
97
) and Fig.
8
.

Furthermore, we notice that the authors of Ref.
Litim et al. 2023
mentioned
that the anomalous dimension
\gamma_{m_{\psi}}
of the fermion mass, which they computed in their paper, is
negative
to the leading order in
\epsilon
, while all the next-to-leading order terms are positive. Because of this,
\gamma_{m_{\psi}}
can become negative and potentially pose a threat to unitarity
Litim et al. 2023
. We argue that this negative leading-order result is nothing else but the leading-order contribution to
(-\gamma_{H})
. Positive higher-order terms computed in Ref.
Litim et al. 2023
cannot be trusted when evaluating scaling dimensions, since at the two-loop level the mixing comes into play.
We account for this mixing in the present study and compute the anomalous-dimension matrix eigenvalues, one of which should be
(-\gamma_{H})
at any loop.
But this is not the end of the story. We also argue that if EOMs are taken into account the anomalous dimension matrix of dimension-three operators is modified
\gamma_{O}\to\tilde{\gamma}_{O}
such that
\tilde{\gamma}_{O}
has the same eigenvalues as
\gamma_{O}
but with the flipped sign of the “dangerous” eigenvalue
(-\gamma_{H})\to\gamma_{H}
. This can be anticipated and represents correct scaling of the operator that enters EOMs alongside with
O_{4}\propto\partial^{2}\Tr(H)
. Our analysis shows that all eigenvalues of the reduced anomalous dimension matrix are positive so dimension-three operators that break
G
flavor symmetry do not spoil unitarity.

We believe that our findings can be used in a more elaborate analysis of the asymptotic safety in the Veneziano limit and beyond the latter. It is interesting how the results will be modified when the 544 order beta functions will be available, e.g., in connection with possible additional FP merger due to potentially negative contribution of
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{4})
to
\theta_{3}
.

We thank Oleg Antipin, Nikita Lebedev, Georgy Kalagov, Dmitry Kazakov, Lukas Mizisin, and Vitaly Velizhanin for fruitful discussions. We also appreciate the correspondence with Tom Steudtner on Ref.
Litim et al. 2023
at the early stage of our study. The work of a A.I.M was supported by the JINR AYSS Foundation, Project No 24-301-02.

Appendix B
Other approximations for finite-
N_{c}
factors

In this appendix we provide the approximations for finite-
N_{c}
factors obtained by fitting numerical data to the ratio of two second-order polynomials in
N_{c}
. The fixed point factors are given as

\displaystyle f_{g}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{c}^{2}}{N_{c}^{2}-\frac{110}{19}},\qquad f_{g}^{(2)}=\frac{N_{c}^{2}+6.750}{N_{c}^{2}-7.669},\qquad f_{g}^{(3)}=\frac{N_{c}^{2}+26.937}{N_{c}^{2}-8.402},
